Twisted products of monoids


Abstract
A twisting of a monoid S S is a map Φ : S × S → ℕ \Phi:S\times S\to\mathbb{N} satisfying the identity Φ ⁡ ( a , b ) + Φ ⁡ ( a ​ b , c ) = Φ ⁡ ( a , b ​ c ) + Φ ⁡ ( b , c ) \Phi(a,b)+\Phi(ab,c)=\Phi(a,bc)+\Phi(b,c) . Together with an additive commutative monoid M M , and a fixed q ∈ M q\in M , this gives rise a so-called twisted product M × Φ q S M\times_{\Phi}^{q}S , which has underlying set M × S M\times S and multiplication ( i , a ) ​ ( j , b ) = ( i + j + Φ ⁡ ( a , b ) ​ q , a ​ b ) (i,a)(j,b)=(i+j+\Phi(a,b)q,ab) . This construction has appeared in the special cases where M M is ℕ \mathbb{N} or ℤ \mathbb{Z} under addition, S S is a diagram monoid (e.g. partition, Brauer or Temperley-Lieb), and Φ \Phi counts floating components in concatenated diagrams. In this paper we identify a special kind of ‘tight’ twisting, and give a thorough structural description of the resulting twisted products. This involves characterising Green’s relations, (von Neumann) regular elements, idempotents, biordered sets, maximal subgroups, Schützenberger groups, and more. We also consider a number of examples, including several apparently new ones, which take as their starting point certain generalisations of Sylvester’s rank inequality from linear algebra. Keywords : Twistings, twisted products, diagram monoids, linear monoids, independence algebras. MSC: 20M10, 20M20.

1
Introduction

Twistings arise in many parts of mathematics, under many different names, such as cocycles and multipliers
[
27
,
14
,
49
,
35
,
12
,
3
,
41
,
10
,
4
,
6
]
. Notably, diagram algebras are twisted semigroup algebras over the corresponding diagram monoids. Let us give an example. If
\Phi(a,b)
denotes the number of ‘floating loops’ when Temperley–Lieb diagrams
a
and
b
are stacked together to form the product
ab
(see Figure
1
), then the following identity holds:

\Phi(a,b)+\Phi(ab,c)=\Phi(a,bc)+\Phi(b,c).

(1.1)

The set of all formal
\mathbb{C}
-linear combinations of such diagrams can then be turned into a twisted semigroup algebra, in which the product of basis elements is given by

a\star b=\xi^{\Phi(a,b)}ab,

for some fixed
\xi\in\mathbb{C}
; this is the Temperley–Lieb algebra
[
51
]
.
Analogous statements hold for other diagram algebras, such as partition, Brauer and Motzkin algebras

[
9
,
44
,
43
,
37
,
7
]
.
This has proved to be a fruitful way to study diagram algebras, for example by creating a link between the cellular structure of the algebra and the ideals and Green’s relations of the monoid
[
52
,
18
,
21
,
17
,
33
,
38
]
.

To mirror the fundamental idea behind twisted algebras, one can define a so-called twisted diagram monoid, with underlying set
\mathbb{N}\times S
, and product

(i,a)(j,b)=(i+j+\Phi(a,b),ab).

Associativity follows from the twisting identity (
1.1
). Twisted diagram monoids have been studied frequently in the literature; see for example
[
26
,
25
,
22
,
8
,
16
,
40
,
15
,
39
,
11
,
5
,
42
]
.
It was advantageous in
[
22
,
39
,
40
]
to embed these into larger twisted monoids with underlying sets
\mathbb{Z}\times S
and the same multiplication. It is in fact possible to change
\mathbb{N}
into an arbitrary commutative monoid
M
, and then for a fixed parameter
q\in M
, define the product

(i,a)(j,b)=(i+j+\Phi(a,b)q,ab),

resulting in what we will call a twisted product,
M\times_{\Phi}^{q}S
.
This can in turn be done for any monoid
S
with a twisting, i.e. a function
\Phi:S\times S\to\mathbb{N}
satisfying (
1.1
), allowing one to capture many more important examples, including transformation monoids, linear monoids, and more general monoids of (partial) endomorphisms/automorphisms of independence algebras.

The purpose of the current paper is to initiate a systematic study of twisted products
M\times_{\Phi}^{q}S
.
Specifically, we will investigate Green’s relations, idempotents, subgroups, and regularity properties.
We obtain the sharpest results by identifying a key ‘tight’ property of a twisting. The above-mentioned ‘loop-counting’ twisting is tight for the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, the planar partition monoid
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
, the Brauer monoid
\mathcal{B}_{n}
and the Temperley–Lieb monoid
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
, but loose for the partial Brauer monoid
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
and the Motzkin monoid
\mathcal{M}_{n}
. The resulting difference in structure can be glimpsed by comparing Figure
3
(middle) to Figure
4
(middle), which respectively show a tight twisted product
M\times_{\Phi}^{q}\mathcal{P}_{2}
and a loose twisted product
M\times_{\Phi}^{q}\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{2}
. Formal explanations of what these diagrams represent will be given below.

a=
b=
=ab

Figure 1:
Stacking Temperley–Lieb diagrams
a
and
b
(left). Here there are two floating loops (shown in red), so
\Phi(a,b)=2
. Removing these components leads to the product
ab
(right).

The article is organised as follows. We begin in Section
2
with preliminary/background material, and then give definitions and basic properties of (tight) twistings and twisted products in Section
3
. We look at a number of examples of twistings in Section
4
, starting with the canonical float-counting twisting of diagram monoids, and then a new family of twistings that we call ‘rigid’.
The latter are based on natural generalisations of Sylvester’s rank inequality from linear algebra, and apply to diagram monoids and to endomorphism monoids of certain independence algebras. Curiously, these rigid twistings are tight for the Brauer monoid, but loose for the other diagram monoids listed above, including the partition and Temperley–Lieb monoids.
We then proceed to discuss the structure of a tight twisted product, specifically by characterising Green’s relations (Section
5
), idempotents and biordered sets (Section
6
), and (von Neumann) regularity (Section
7
). The results of these three sections are fully general, in the sense that they apply to any tight twisted product. We conclude in Section
8
by considering a problem that
crucially depends on the actual twisted product under consideration,
namely the determination of the idempotent-generated submonoid. We completely describe these submonoids for rigid twisted products over groups, and for partition monoids with respect to the canonical twisting, and also compare these to existing results in the literature.

2
Preliminaries

We now give the preliminary definitions we need concerning semigroups (Section
2.1
), transformation and diagram monoids (Section
2.2
) and independence algebras (Section
2.3
). For more details, see for example
[
36
,
13
,
21
,
31
]
.
Throughout the paper we write
\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
and
\mathbb{Z}=\{0,\pm 1,\pm 2,\ldots\}
for the sets of natural numbers and integers, respectively.

2.1
Semigroups

Let
S
be a semigroup, and denote by
S^{1}
its monoid completion. That is,
S^{1}=S
if
S
is a monoid, or else
S=S\cup\{1\}
, where
1\not\in S
acts as an adjoined identity element. Green’s
\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}
,
\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}
and
\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}
pre-orders and equivalences are defined, for
a,b\in S
, by

\displaystyle a\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}}b

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow\ S^{1}a\subseteq S^{1}b,

\displaystyle a\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}b

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow\ S^{1}a=S^{1}b,

\displaystyle a\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}}b

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow\ aS^{1}\subseteq bS^{1},

\displaystyle a\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}b

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow\ aS^{1}=bS^{1},

\displaystyle a\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}b

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow\ S^{1}aS^{1}\subseteq S^{1}bS^{1},

\displaystyle a\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}b

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow\ S^{1}aS^{1}=S^{1}bS^{1}.

From these, we can also define the pre-order
{\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}}}={\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}}}\cap{\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}}}
, and the equivalences
{\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}}={\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}}\cap{\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}}
and
{\operatorname{\mathscr{D}}}={\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}}\vee{\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}}
. The latter denotes the join of
\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}
and
\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}
in the lattice of all equivalence relations of
S
, i.e. the transitive closure of
{\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}}\cup{\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}}
. It is well known that
{\operatorname{\mathscr{D}}}={\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}}\circ{\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}}={\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}}\circ{\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}}
(where
\circ
is the usual composition operation on binary relations), and that
{\operatorname{\mathscr{D}}}={\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}
when
S
is finite. If
S
is commutative, then all five of Green’s equivalences are equal, i.e.
{\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}}={\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}}={\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}={\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}}={\operatorname{\mathscr{D}}}
, and similarly the four pre-orders are equal.

If
\operatorname{\mathscr{K}}
is any of
\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}
,
\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}
,
\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}
,
\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}
or
\operatorname{\mathscr{D}}
, we denote by
K_{a}
the
\operatorname{\mathscr{K}}
-class of an element
a\in S
. If
{\operatorname{\mathscr{K}}}\not={\operatorname{\mathscr{D}}}
, then the set
S/{\operatorname{\mathscr{K}}}
of all such
\operatorname{\mathscr{K}}
-classes is partially ordered by

K_{a}\leq K_{b}\ \Leftrightarrow\ a\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{K}}}b\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $a,b\in S$.}

Aspects of the structure of a finite semigroup can be conveniently visualised by using a so-called
egg-box diagram
. Elements of each
{\operatorname{\mathscr{D}}}={\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}
-class are drawn in a rectangular array, with rows containing
\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}
-related elements, columns containing
\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}
-related elements, and hence cells (intersections of rows and columns) containing
\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}
-related elements. The
\leq
-relationships between
{\operatorname{\mathscr{D}}}={\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}
classes are also indicated as a Hasse diagram. Several examples can be seen in Figures
3
–
5
.

An element
a
of a semigroup
S
is (von Neumann)
regular
if
a\in aSa
, i.e. if
a=aba
for some
b\in S
.
Note then that the element
c=bab
satisfies
a=aca
and
c=cac
; in this case,
a
and
c
are said to be (semigroup)
inverses
of each other. If
a
is regular, then so too is every element of its
\operatorname{\mathscr{D}}
-class
D_{a}
. We write
\operatorname{Reg}(S)
for the set of all regular elements of
S
, and we say that
S
is regular if
S=\operatorname{Reg}(S)
. The semigroup
S
is said to be
inverse
if every element
a
has a unique inverse, denoted
a^{-1}
; it is well known that
a\mapsto a^{-1}
is an
involution
of
S
, meaning that
(a^{-1})^{-1}=a
and
(ab)^{-1}=b^{-1}a^{-1}
for all
a,b\in S
.

An
idempotent
of a semigroup
S
is an element
e\in S
satisfying
e=e^{2}
. The set of all idempotents is denoted
E(S)
. The
\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}
-class of any idempotent is a group. All group
\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}
-classes contained in a common
\operatorname{\mathscr{D}}
-class are isomorphic. If
D
is a regular
\operatorname{\mathscr{D}}
-class, then every
\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}
-class contained in
D
contains at least one idempotent, and similarly for the
\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}
-classes in
D
.
In egg-box diagrams, cells containing idempotents (i.e. the group
\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}
-classes) are shaded grey; again see Figures
3
–
5
.
When
S
is a monoid with identity
1
, the
\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}
-class
H_{1}
is called the
group of units
of
S
.
We say that
S
is
unipotent
if
E(S)=\{1\}
.

2.2
Transformation and diagram monoids

For a set
X
, we denote by

\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
the
partial transformation monoid
,

\mathcal{T}_{X}
the
full transformation monoid
,

\mathcal{I}_{X}
the
symmetric inverse monoid
, and

\mathcal{S}_{X}
the
symmetric group
.
These consist, respectively, of all partial transformations
X\to X
, full transformations
X\to X
, partial bijections
X\to X
, and bijections
X\to X
, under composition in each case. All four monoids are regular, and moreover
\mathcal{I}_{X}
is inverse and
\mathcal{S}_{X}
is a group. When
X=\{1,\ldots,n\}
for some integer
n\geq 1
, we write
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, and similarly for
\mathcal{T}_{n}
,
\mathcal{I}_{n}
and
\mathcal{S}_{n}
.

A further key class of examples are the
diagram monoids
, which are all submonoids of the partition monoids, to which we now turn. Fix a positive integer
n
, and write
{\bf n}=\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
{{\bf n}^{\prime}=\{1^{\prime},\ldots,n^{\prime}\}}
. The
partition monoid

\mathcal{P}_{n}
consists of all set partitions of
{\bf n}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime}
. A partition
a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
is identified with any graph on vertex set
{\bf n}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime}
whose connected components are the blocks of
a
; such a graph is typically drawn with vertices
1<\cdots<n
on an upper row and
1^{\prime}<\cdots<n^{\prime}
on a lower row. Some example partitions from
\mathcal{P}_{6}
are shown in Figure
2
:

\begin{split}a=\big\{\{1,4\},\{2,3,4^{\prime},5^{\prime}\},\{5,6\},\{1^{\prime},2^{\prime},6^{\prime}\},\{3^{\prime}\}\big\},\\
b=\big\{\{1,2\},\{3,4,1^{\prime}\},\{5,4^{\prime},5^{\prime},6^{\prime}\},\{6\},\{2^{\prime},3^{\prime}\}\big\}.\end{split}

(2.1)

The product of partitions
a,b\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
is calculated as follows. First, let
{\bf n}^{\prime\prime}=\{1^{\prime\prime},\ldots,n^{\prime\prime}\}
, and define three further graphs:

•

a^{\vee}
on vertex set
{\bf n}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime\prime}
, obtained by changing each lower vertex
x^{\prime}
of
a
to
x^{\prime\prime}
,

•

b^{\wedge}
on vertex set
{\bf n}^{\prime\prime}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime}
, obtained by changing each upper vertex
x
of
b
to
x^{\prime\prime}
,

•

\Pi(a,b)
on vertex set
{\bf n}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime\prime}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime}
, whose edge set is the union of those of
a^{\vee}
and
b^{\wedge}
.

The graph
\Pi(a,b)
is called the
product graph
of
a
and
b
; the vertices
1^{\prime\prime}<\cdots<n^{\prime\prime}
are drawn in the middle row.
The product
ab\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
is then defined to be the partition for which
x,y\in{\bf n}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime}
belong to the same block of
ab
if and only if
x
and
y
belong to the same connected component of
\Pi(a,b)
. Calculation of the product
ab
in
\mathcal{P}_{6}
, with
a
and
b
as in (
2.1
), is shown in Figure
2
.

Note that the product graph
\Pi(a,b)
may contain
floating components
, by which we mean components consisting entirely of double-dashed elements. The number
\Phi(a,b)
of such floating components will play a key role throughout. For example, if
a,b\in\mathcal{P}_{6}
are as in (
2.1
), then
{\Phi(a,b)=1}
, with the unique floating component of
\Pi(a,b)
being
\{1^{\prime\prime},2^{\prime\prime},6^{\prime\prime}\}
; see Figure
2
.

a=
b=
=ab

Figure 2:
Multiplication of partitions
a,b\in\mathcal{P}_{6}
, with the product graph
\Pi(a,b)
in the middle.

The partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
contains many important submonoids, including:

•

the
Brauer monoid

\mathcal{B}_{n}=\{a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}:\text{each block of $a$ has size $2$}\}
,

•

the
partial Brauer monoid

\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}=\{a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}:\text{each block of $a$ has size $\leq 2$}\}
,

•

the
planar partition monoid

\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}=\{a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}:\text{$a$ is planar}\}
,
where planarity here means that
a
can be drawn with no crossings, with all edges contained in the rectangle bounded by the vertices,

•

the
Temperley–Lieb monoid

\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}=\mathcal{B}_{n}\cap\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
, and

•

the
Motzkin monoid

\mathcal{M}_{n}=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}\cap\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
.

An example of a planar partition is
b\in\mathcal{P}_{6}
pictured in Figure
2
.

A block
A
of a partition
a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
is called
an
upper
or
lower non-transversal
if
A\subseteq{\bf n}
or
A\subseteq{\bf n}^{\prime}
, respectively.
Any other block is a
transversal
.
We write

a=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&D_{1}&\cdots&D_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)

to indicate that
a
has transversals
A_{i}\cup B_{i}^{\prime}
(for
1\leq i\leq r
), upper non-transversals
C_{i}
(for
1\leq i\leq s
) and lower non-transversals
D_{i}^{\prime}
(for
1\leq i\leq t
). Any of
r
,
s
or
t
could be
0
, but not all three since we are assuming that
n\geq 1
.

The partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
comes equipped with a natural involution
∗
, defined by turning diagrams up-side down:

a=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&D_{1}&\cdots&D_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\mapsto\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&D_{1}&\cdots&D_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)=a^{*}.

This satisfies the identities
a^{**}=a=aa^{*}a
and
(ab)^{*}=b^{*}a^{*}
, so that
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is a
regular
*
-monoid

[
48
]
. In particular,
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is regular, but it is not inverse for
n\geq 2
. The submonoids
\mathcal{B}_{n}
,
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
,
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
and
\mathcal{M}_{n}
are all closed under
∗
, so they are also regular
*
-monoids.

The (
co
)
domain
, (
co
)
kernel
and
rank
of a partition
a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
are defined by

\displaystyle\operatorname{dom}(a)

\displaystyle=\{x\in{\bf n}:x\text{ is contained in a transversal of }a\},

\displaystyle\operatorname{codom}(a)

\displaystyle=\{x\in{\bf n}:x^{\prime}\text{ is contained in a transversal of }a\},

\displaystyle\ker(a)

\displaystyle=\{(x,y)\in{\bf n}\times{\bf n}:\text{$x$ and $y$ belong to the same block of $a$}\},

\displaystyle\operatorname{coker}(a)

\displaystyle=\{(x,y)\in{\bf n}\times{\bf n}:\text{$x^{\prime}$ and $y^{\prime}$ belong to the same block of $a$}\},

\displaystyle\operatorname{rank}(a)

\displaystyle=\text{the number of transversals of $a$}.

For example,
a\in\mathcal{P}_{6}
in Figure
2
has rank
1
, domain
\{2,3\}
, and kernel-classes
\{1,4\}
,
\{2,3\}
and
\{5,6\}
.

The above parameters can be used to characterise Green’s relations on any of the above diagram monoids. In particular, it will be useful to bear in mind the following:

•

a\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ [\operatorname{dom}(a)=\operatorname{dom}(b)\text{ and }\ker(a)=\ker(b)]
,

•

a\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ [\operatorname{codom}(a)=\operatorname{codom}(b)\text{ and }\operatorname{coker}(a)=\operatorname{coker}(b)]
,

•

a\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ \operatorname{rank}(a)=\operatorname{rank}(b)
,

•

a\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ \operatorname{rank}(a)\leq\operatorname{rank}(b)
.

For full details, see
[
42
,
52
]
. In particular, if
S
is any of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
,
\mathcal{B}_{n}
,
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{M}_{n}
or
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
, then the
{\operatorname{\mathscr{D}}}={\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}
-classes of
S
are the sets

D_{r}=D_{r}(S)=\{a\in S:\operatorname{rank}(a)=r\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $0\leq r\leq n$,}

with the additional constraint that
r\equiv n
(mod
2
) if
S
is either
\mathcal{B}_{n}
or
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
. The ordering on these is given by
D_{r}\leq D_{s}\ \Leftrightarrow\ r\leq s
.

Finally, we note that the transformation monoids
\mathcal{T}_{n}
and
\mathcal{I}_{n}
(and hence also
\mathcal{S}_{n}
) can be regarded as submonoids of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
:

\mathcal{T}_{n}=\{a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}:\operatorname{dom}(a)={\bf n}\text{ and }\ker(a)=\Delta_{\bf n}\}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{I}_{n}=\{a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}:\ker(a)=\Delta_{\bf n}=\operatorname{coker}(a)\}.

Here and elsewhere we write
\Delta_{X}=\{(x,x):x\in X\}
for the trivial/equality relation on a set
X
. Note that
\mathcal{I}_{n}
is closed under the involution
∗
(which restricts to the inverse operation on
\mathcal{I}_{n}
), but
\mathcal{T}_{n}
is not.

2.3
Independence algebras and their (partial) endomorphism monoids

Another source of examples for us will be certain twistings on semigroups of transformations and matrices. They can in fact be treated within a common framework of endomorphism monoids of independence algebras. These algebras were first introduced by Narkiewicz
[
47
]
under the name of
v^{*}
-algebras. The study of their endomorphisms, and indeed the term ‘independence algebras’, were introduced by Gould
[
31
]
. For more details on the history of this topic, and connections with other areas of mathematics, see
[
1
]
.

Let
A
be an algebra (in the sense of universal algebra). An
endomorphism
of
A
is a morphism
A\to A
, i.e. a self-map of
A
that respects the operations of
A
. A
partial endomorphism
of
A
is a morphism
B\to A
for some subalgebra
B\leq A
. The sets
\operatorname{End}(A)
and
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
of all such (partial) endomorphisms are monoids under composition. So too is
{\operatorname{PAut}(A)=\{a\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A):a\text{ is injective}\}}
, and the latter is inverse. The automorphism group
\operatorname{Aut}(A)=\{a\in\operatorname{End}(A):a\text{ is bijective}\}
is the group of units of all three of
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
,
\operatorname{End}(A)
and
\operatorname{PAut}(A)
.

For a subset
X
of an algebra
A
we write
\langle X\rangle
for the subalgebra generated by
X
. We say
X
is
independent
if
x\not\in\langle X\setminus\{x\}\rangle
for any
x\in X
. A
basis
of
A
is an independent generating set.

Following
[
31
]
, an
independence algebra
is an algebra
A
that satisfies the
exchange property
and the
free basis property
:

(E)

For all
X\subseteq A
and
x,y\in A
, if
x\in\langle X\cup\{y\}\rangle\setminus\langle X\rangle
, then
y\in\langle X\cup\{x\}\rangle
.

(F)

If
X
is a basis for
A
, then any map
X\to A
can be extended (necessarily uniquely) to an endomorphism of
A
.

It can be shown that any independent set of an independence algebra
A
can be extended to a basis, and that all bases have the same size, called the
dimension
of
A
and denoted
\dim(A)
. It follows that
(F)
is equivalent to the ostensibly stronger condition:

(F)
′

If
X\subseteq A
is independent, then any map
X\to A
can be extended (necessarily uniquely) to a partial endomorphism
\langle X\rangle\to A
.

The
rank
of a partial endomorphism
a\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
is defined by
\operatorname{rank}(a)=\dim(\operatorname{im}(a))
, the dimension of the image of
a
. (Note that any subalgebra of an independence algebra is itself an independence algebra, and therefore has a well-defined dimension.)

In our results we will need to assume that the underlying independence algebras are
strong
.
This is a concept introduced by Fountain and Lewin in their work
[
29
]
on endomorphism monoids of independence algebras.
It formalises a well-known property of vector spaces, which turns out not to follow from the defining properties of an independence algebra. Fountain and Lewin give several equivalent formulations, of which we will work with the following
(see
[
29
, Lemma 1.6]
):

(S)

If
B,C\leq A
are subalgebras, and if a basis
X
for
B\cap C
is extended to bases
X\sqcup Y
and
X\sqcup Z
for
B
and
C
, respectively, then
X\sqcup Y\sqcup Z
is a basis for
B\vee C
.

Here
\sqcup
denotes disjoint union, and
B\vee C=\langle B\cup C\rangle
is the join of
B
and
C
in the lattice of subalgebras of
A
.

Archetypal examples of strong independence algebras include vector spaces and (plain) sets. (Note that the former have a separate unary operation for scalar multiplication by each element of the underlying field.) If
A
is simply a set, then

\operatorname{PEnd}(A)=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{A},\hskip 20.00003pt\operatorname{End}(A)=\mathcal{T}_{A},\hskip 20.00003pt\operatorname{PAut}(A)=\mathcal{I}_{A}\and\operatorname{Aut}(A)=\mathcal{S}_{A}.

If
A
is a vector space over a field
F
, of finite dimension
n
, then
\operatorname{End}(A)
is isomorphic to
M_{n}(F)
, the multiplicative monoid of all
n\times n
matrices over
F
. Structural similarities between the monoids
\mathcal{T}_{n}
and
M_{n}(F)
were one source of interest in independence algebras in
[
31
]
. During the course of our investigations, we will uncover an intriguing point of difference between these two monoids, namely that a certain ‘rank-based twisting’ is ‘tight’ for
M_{n}(F)
but not for
\mathcal{T}_{n}
; see Proposition
4.13
and Remark
4.12
.

3
Twistings: definitions and basic properties

Throughout this section we fix the following:

•

a monoid
S
, written multiplicatively, with identity
1
; and

•

a commutative monoid
M
, written additively, with identity
0
.

Definition 3.1
.

A
twisting
of
S
is a map
\Phi:S\times S\to\mathbb{N}
satisfying the following:

(T1)

\Phi(a,b)+\Phi(ab,c)=\Phi(a,bc)+\Phi(b,c)
for all
a,b,c\in S
.

With a slight abuse of notation we write
\Phi(a,b,c)
for the common value in
(T1)
.

In the work on congruences of twisted partition monoids
[
26
,
25
]
it transpired that the following property of multiplication in
\mathcal{P}_{n}
was important in a number of technical arguments: for any partitions
a,b\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
the product
ab
can be obtained without introducing any floating components in the product graph by replacing
b
by a suitable
b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, and, dually, replacing
a
by a suitable
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
. Formalising to our general framework leads to the following:

Definition 3.2
.

A twisting
\Phi
is
tight
if it satisfies the following:

(T2)

(a)

For all
a,b\in S
there exist
a^{\prime}\in S
such that
ab=a^{\prime}b
and
\Phi(a^{\prime},b)=0
.

(b)

For all
a,b\in S
there exist
b^{\prime}\in S
such that
ab=ab^{\prime}
and
\Phi(a,b^{\prime})=0
.

Otherwise
\Phi
is
loose
.

One can of course obtain a (tight) twisting by defining
\Phi(a,b)=0
for all
a,b\in S
. We call this the
trivial
twisting.
Given a twisting
\Phi
and a natural number
k
, we can define a new twisting
\Phi^{\prime}
by
\Phi^{\prime}(a,b)=k+\Phi(a,b)
; if
k>0
then this is loose.
If
T
is a subsemigroup of
S
, then any twisting
\Phi
of
S
restricts to a twisting of
T
. If
\Phi
is tight, then the restricted twisting need not be tight in general; similarly, a loose twisting could restrict to a tight one.
Several examples of twistings will be considered in Section
4
.
In particular, in Remark
4.10
we will see an example where
(T2)
(T2)(b)
holds, but
(T2)
(T2)(a)
does not.

Here is the key definition, using a twisting to combine
M
and
S
into a single semigroup:

Definition 3.3
.

Let
\Phi:S\times S\to\mathbb{N}
be a twisting, and fix some
q\in M
. The
twisted product

M\times_{\Phi}^{q}S
is defined to be the semigroup with underlying set
M\times S
, and operation

(i,a)(j,b)=(i+j+\Phi(a,b)q,ab)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $i,j\in M$ and $a,b\in S$.}

The product
M\times_{\Phi}^{q}S
is said to be
tight
if
\Phi
is tight, or
loose
otherwise.

Associativity of the above operation follows quickly from
(T1)
, and in fact we have

(i,a)(j,b)(k,c)=(i+j+k+\Phi(a,b,c)q,abc)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $i,j,k\in M$ and $a,b,c\in S$.}

Note that if
\Phi
is the trivial twisting, or if
q=0
, then
M\times_{\Phi}^{q}S
is the classical direct product
M\times S
, with operation
(i,a)(j,b)=(i+j,ab)
.

The following lemma gathers some basic consequences of
(T1)
and
(T2)
.

Lemma 3.4
.

If
\Phi:S\times S\to\mathbb{N}
is a tight twisting, then for all
a,b,c\in S
, the following hold:

(T3)

a\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}}b\ \Rightarrow\ \Phi(a,c)\geq\Phi(b,c)
, and
a\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}b\ \Rightarrow\ \Phi(a,c)=\Phi(b,c)
,

(T4)

a\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}}b\ \Rightarrow\ \Phi(c,a)\geq\Phi(c,b)
, and
a\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}b\ \Rightarrow\ \Phi(c,a)=\Phi(c,b)
,

(T5)

\Phi(1,a)=\Phi(a,1)=0
,

(T6)

there exist
a^{\prime},c^{\prime}\in S
such that
abc=a^{\prime}bc^{\prime}
and
\Phi(a^{\prime},b,c^{\prime})=0
.

Proof.

(T3)
. For the first implication, suppose
a\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}}b
. Then
a=sb
for some
s\in S
, and by
(T2)
we can assume that
\Phi(s,b)=0
. Combining this with
(T1)
, we have

\Phi(a,c)=0+\Phi(a,c)=\Phi(s,b)+\Phi(sb,c)=\Phi(s,bc)+\Phi(b,c)\geq\Phi(b,c).

The second implication follows from the first.

(T4)
. This is dual to
(T3)
.

(T5)
. By
(T2)
, there exists
a^{\prime}\in M
such that
a\cdot 1=a^{\prime}\cdot 1
and
\Phi(a^{\prime},1)=0
. But
a\cdot 1=a^{\prime}\cdot 1
simply says that
a=a^{\prime}
, so indeed
\Phi(a,1)=\Phi(a^{\prime},1)=0
. We obtain
\Phi(1,a)=0
by symmetry.

(T6)
. By
(T2)
there exist
a^{\prime},c^{\prime}\in S
such that
ab=a^{\prime}b
and
a^{\prime}b\cdot c=a^{\prime}b\cdot c^{\prime}
, with
\Phi(a^{\prime},b)=0
and
\Phi(a^{\prime}b,c^{\prime})=0
. We then have

a^{\prime}bc^{\prime}=a^{\prime}bc=abc\and\Phi(a^{\prime},b,c^{\prime})=\Phi(a^{\prime},b)+\Phi(a^{\prime}b,c^{\prime})=0+0=0.\qed

We now list some fundamental properties of a tight twisted product
T=M\times_{\Phi}^{q}S
, which we will use without explicit reference. The third is easily checked, and the rest are simple consequences of
(T5)
.

(P1)

T
is a monoid with identity
(0,1)
.

(P2)

The set
M\times\{1\}
is a submonoid of
T
, and is isomorphic to
M
.

(P3)

If
M
has an absorbing element
\infty
(so
i+\infty=\infty
for all
i\in M
), then
\{\infty\}\times S
is a submonoid of
T
, and is isomorphic to
S
. (This holds more generally if
\infty
is replaced by any element
w\in M
for which
2w=w=w+q
.)

(P4)

T
is generated by the union of
M\times\{1\}
and
\{0\}\times S
. Specifically, we have

(i,a)=(i,1)(0,a)=(0,a)(i,1)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for any $i\in M$ and $a\in S$.}

While the subset
\{0\}\times S
of
T
is in one-one correspondence with
S
, it is typically not a submonoid, as
(0,a)(0,b)=(\Phi(a,b)q,ab)
for
a,b\in S
.

The next result will be used when we consider idempotents in Section
6
.

Lemma 3.5
.

If
\Phi
is a twisting of a monoid
S
, and if
a\in S
and
e\in E(S)
, then

a\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}}e\ \Rightarrow\ \Phi(a,e)=\Phi(e,e)\and a\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}}e\ \Rightarrow\ \Phi(e,a)=\Phi(e,e).

Proof.

For the first implication (the second is dual), note that
a\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}}e
implies
a=ae
. Combining this with
(T1)
yields

\Phi(a,e)+\Phi(a,e)=\Phi(a,e)+\Phi(ae,e)=\Phi(a,ee)+\Phi(e,e)=\Phi(a,e)+\Phi(e,e),

and the assertion follows.
∎

Some of our motivating examples involve semigroups
S
with an involution
∗
. We say that a twisting
\Phi
of such a semigroup is
*
-symmetric
if

\Phi(a,b)=\Phi(b^{*},a^{*})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $a,b\in S$.}

(3.6)

Lemma 3.7
.

If
\Phi
is a
*
-symmetric twisting of a monoid
S
with involution, then
(T2)
(T2)(a)
and
(T2)
(T2)(b)
are equivalent. Consequently,
\Phi
is tight if and only if either of these holds.

Proof.

It suffices by symmetry to show that
(T2)
(T2)(a)
implies
(T2)
(T2)(b)
, so suppose
(T2)
(T2)(a)
holds. To verify
(T2)
(T2)(b)
, let
a,b\in S
. By assumption there exists
c\in S
such that
b^{*}a^{*}=ca^{*}
and
\Phi(c,a^{*})=0
. We then take
b^{\prime}=c^{*}
, and we note that

ab^{\prime}=ac^{*}=(ca^{*})^{*}=(b^{*}a^{*})^{*}=ab\and\Phi(a,b^{\prime})=\Phi(a,c^{*})=\Phi(c,a^{*})=0.\qed

4
Motivating examples

The purpose of this section is to describe some natural examples of twistings. These include the canonical twistings of diagram monoids (Section
4.1
), a new twisting for monoids of partial endomorphisms of strong independence algebras (Section
4.3
), including full transformation monoids and matrix monoids, and analogous new twistings for diagram monoids (Section
4.4
).

4.1
Diagram monoids and their canonical twistings

As just noted, our first motivating example concerns diagram monoids, and we begin with the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
.
For partitions
a,b\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, we let
\Phi(a,b)
be the number of floating components in the product graph
\Pi(a,b)
. It is a non-trivial fact that this
\Phi
is a tight twisting. Properties
(T1)
and
(T2)
are
[
28
, Lemma 4.1]
and
[
25
, Lemma 2.9(i)]
, respectively. We call this
\Phi
the
canonical twisting
of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
. It is clear from the definition that
\Phi
is
*
-symmetric with respect to the standard involution
∗
of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
.

Taking
M
to be either
(\mathbb{N},+)
or
(\mathbb{Z},+)
, and
q=1
in either case, leads to two twisted products:

\mathbb{N}\times_{\Phi}^{1}\mathcal{P}_{n}
and
\mathbb{Z}\times_{\Phi}^{1}\mathcal{P}_{n}
.
The first of these monoids was the subject of the papers
[
26
,
25
]
.
The second has not been explicitly studied, to the best of our knowledge, but will play a pivotal role in the forthcoming paper
[
22
]
.

Of course
\Phi
restricts to a twisting of any submonoid of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
. Curiously, this restriction is not always tight, as we will see in Proposition
4.2
. For the proof we require a technical lemma about planar partitions:

Lemma 4.1
.

For any
a\in\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
with
\operatorname{rank}(a)\geq 1
, there exists
c\in\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
such that
ac\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}a
,
\operatorname{codom}(ac)={\bf n}
and
\Phi(a,c)=0
.

Proof.

If
r=1
we can simply take
c=\binom{{\bf n}}{{\bf n}}
, the partition with a single block. Now suppose
r\geq 2
, and write
a=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&D_{1}&\cdots&D_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
. By
[
24
, Lemma 7.1]
we can assume that
{B_{1}<\cdots<B_{r}}
(where
X<Y
means that
x<y
for all
x\in X
and
y\in Y
). Write
m_{i}=\max(B_{i})
for each
i
, and define the sets

M_{1}=\{1,\ldots,m_{1}\},\hskip 10.00002ptM_{i}=\{m_{i-1}+1,\ldots,m_{i}\}\text{ for $1<i<r$}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptM_{r}=\{m_{r-1}+1,\ldots,n\}.

Then the conditions are easily checked for
c=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil M_{1}&\cdots&M_{r}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil M_{1}&\cdots&M_{r}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
, noting that
ac=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil M_{1}&\cdots&M_{r}&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
.
∎

Proposition 4.2
.

The canonical twisting is tight for
\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{B}_{n}
,
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
and
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
, but loose for
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
and
\mathcal{M}_{n}
if
n\geq 2
.

Proof.

Property
(T2)
for
\mathcal{B}_{n}
and
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
is established in
[
20
]
.

For
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
, it suffices by Lemma
3.7
to establish
(T2)
(T2)(b)
. To do so, let
{a,b\in\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}}
. Suppose first that
\operatorname{rank}(a)=0
, and let the lower non-transversals of
ab
be
A_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,A_{k}^{\prime}
, where we assume that
1\in A_{1}
. Then
ab=ab^{\prime}
for
b^{\prime}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil{\bf n}&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&A_{2}&\cdots&A_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\in\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
, and we have
\Phi(a,b^{\prime})=0
because
\operatorname{dom}(b^{\prime})={\bf n}
.
Now suppose
\operatorname{rank}(a)\geq 1
, and let
c\in\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
be as in Lemma
4.1
. Now,
{ab\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}}a\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}ac}
, so we have
ab=acd
for some
d\in\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
. Thus, it remains to check that
\Phi(a,b^{\prime})=0
for
b^{\prime}=cd
. Now,

\Phi(a,c,d)=\Phi(a,c)+\Phi(ac,d)=0,

as
\Phi(a,c)=0
, and
\Phi(ac,d)=0
since
\operatorname{codom}(ac)={\bf n}
. But then it follows that

\Phi(a,b^{\prime})=\Phi(a,cd)\leq\Phi(a,cd)+\Phi(c,d)=\Phi(a,c,d)=0,

so certainly
\Phi(a,b^{\prime})=0
.

For the failure of
(T2)
(T2)(b)
in
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
and
\mathcal{M}_{n}
, take

a={\lower 6.02773pt\hbox{

(4.3)

both from
\mathcal{M}_{n}
. Then

, and the only other

is

, but we have
\Phi(a,b)=1
and
\Phi(a,b^{\prime})=2
.
∎

The products
\mathbb{N}\times_{\Phi}^{1}\mathcal{B}_{n}
and
\mathbb{N}\times_{\Phi}^{1}\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
have appeared frequently in the literature
[
8
,
16
,
40
,
15
,
39
,
11
,
5
,
42
]
, with the latter typically called the
Kauffman monoid
. The larger monoids
\mathbb{Z}\times_{\Phi}^{1}\mathcal{B}_{n}
and
\mathbb{Z}\times_{\Phi}^{1}\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
have also featured in
[
39
,
40
]
. Twisted products involving
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
and
\mathcal{M}_{n}
have more intricate structures, due to their twistings being loose. For example, the middle diagrams in Figures
3
and
4
show twisted products involving
\mathcal{P}_{2}
and
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{2}
, respectively, with respect to the canonical twistings; the former is tight, and the latter is loose.

4.2
Rigid twistings

The remaining twistings we consider here arise from a general construction:

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
S
be a monoid,
r:S\to\mathbb{Z}
an arbitrary function, and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
an arbitrary integer. Then the function

\Phi_{r,m}:S\times S\to\mathbb{Z}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{given by}\hskip 20.00003pt\Phi_{r,m}(a,b)=m-r(a)-r(b)+r(ab)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $a,b\in S$}

satisfies
(T1)
. Consequently,
\Phi_{r,m}
is a twisting of
S
if and only if the following inequality holds:

r(a)+r(b)\leq r(ab)+m\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $a,b\in S$.}

(4.5)

If additionally
S
has an involution
∗
for which the identity
r(a^{*})=r(a)
holds, then
\Phi_{r,m}
is
*
-symmetric.

Proof.

It is easy to check that both sides of
(T1)
evaluate to
2m-r(a)-r(b)-r(c)+r(abc)
.
It is also clear that the inequality (
4.5
) is equivalent to having
\Phi_{r,m}(a,b)\geq 0
for all
a,b\in S
.
Finally, if the identity
r(a^{*})=r(a)
holds, then for any
a,b\in S
we have

\Phi_{r,m}(b^{*},a^{*})=m-r(b^{*})-r(a^{*})+r(b^{*}a^{*})=m-r(b)-r(a)+r(ab)=\Phi_{r,m}(a,b).\qed

A twisting of the above form
\Phi_{r,m}
will be called
rigid
. Note that

\Phi_{r,m}(a,1)=\Phi_{r,m}(1,a)=m-r(1)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for any $a\in S$.}

In particular, if
r(1)=m
, then
M\times_{\Phi_{r,m}}^{q}S
is a monoid with identity
(0,1)
, whether
\Phi_{r,m}
is tight or not. Note also that
r(1)=m
is a necessary condition for a rigid twisting
\Phi_{r,m}
to be tight.

Rigid twistings can be thought of as generalisations of the
coboundaries
from the paper
[
14
]
, which studied analogues of twistings that map from a cancellative commutative monoid to a group (rather than from an arbitrary monoid to
\mathbb{N}
). The main result of
[
14
]
was that every twisting of the type they study is a coboundary.
This is not the case for twistings in general. In particular, we will see later that the canonical twistings on diagram monoids are not rigid; see Remark
4.19
.

4.3
(Partial) endomorphism monoids of strong independence algabras

A special case of (
4.5
) is the well-known
Sylvester rank inequality
in linear algebra, which says that for
n\times n
matrices
a
and
b
over a field
F
we have

\operatorname{rank}(a)+\operatorname{rank}(b)\leq\operatorname{rank}(ab)+n.

Because of Lemma
4.4
, this leads to a rigid twisting on the multiplicative monoid
M_{n}(F)
of all such matrices, given by
\Phi(a,b)=n-\operatorname{rank}(a)-\operatorname{rank}(b)+\operatorname{rank}(ab)
. Among other things, we will see that this twisting is tight; see Proposition
4.13
.

In fact, Sylvester’s rank inequality holds in much greater generality, namely for partial endomorphisms of finite-dimensional strong independence algebras:

Proposition 4.6
.

If
A
is a strong independence algebra of finite dimension
n
, then

\operatorname{rank}(a)+\operatorname{rank}(b)\leq\operatorname{rank}(ab)+n\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $a,b\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)$.}

Proof.

Write
k=\operatorname{rank}(a)
and
l=\operatorname{rank}(ab)
. We must show that

\operatorname{rank}(b)\leq l+n-k.

(4.7)

Also define the subalgebras

B=\operatorname{im}(a)\and C=\operatorname{dom}(b).

Choose a basis
\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{l}\}
for
\operatorname{im}(ab)
, and choose
u_{1},\ldots,u_{l}\in A
such that
u_{i}ab=w_{i}
for each
i
. Also set
v_{i}=u_{i}a
for each
i
. Since
\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{l}\}
is independent, so too are
\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{l}\}
and
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{l}\}
, and we note that
\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{l}\}\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(a)
and
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{l}\}\subseteq\operatorname{im}(a)\cap\operatorname{dom}(b)=B\cap C
. Next we extend
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{l}\}
to bases:

•

\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}\}=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{l}\}\cup\{v_{l+1},\ldots,v_{m}\}
of
B\cap C
,

•

\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}\}\cup\{v_{m+1},\ldots,v_{k}\}
of
B
, and

•

\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}\}\cup\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{h}\}
of
C=\operatorname{dom}(b)
.

Now,
\operatorname{im}(b)
is generated by the image of this last basis, meaning that

\operatorname{im}(b)=\langle v_{1}b,\ldots,v_{m}b,x_{1}b,\ldots,x_{h}b\rangle=\langle v_{1}b,\ldots,v_{m}b\rangle\vee\langle x_{1}b,\ldots,x_{h}b\rangle.

It follows that

\operatorname{rank}(b)=\dim(\operatorname{im}(b))\leq\dim\langle v_{1}b,\ldots,v_{m}b\rangle+\dim\langle x_{1}b,\ldots,x_{h}b\rangle\leq\dim\langle v_{1}b,\ldots,v_{m}b\rangle+h.

Since
v_{i}\in B\cap C=\operatorname{im}(a)\cap\operatorname{dom}(b)
for each
1\leq i\leq m
, we have

\langle v_{1}b,\ldots,v_{m}b\rangle\subseteq\operatorname{im}(ab)=\langle w_{1},\ldots,w_{l}\rangle=\langle v_{1}b,\ldots,v_{l}b\rangle\subseteq\langle v_{1}b,\ldots,v_{m}b\rangle.

It follows that
\langle v_{1}b,\ldots,v_{m}b\rangle=\operatorname{im}(ab)
, and so
\dim\langle v_{1}b,\ldots,v_{m}b\rangle=\operatorname{rank}(ab)=l
. Thus, continuing from above, we have

\operatorname{rank}(b)\leq\dim\langle v_{1}b,\ldots,v_{m}b\rangle+h=l+h.

Thus, we can complete the proof of (
4.7
), and hence of the proposition, by showing that
h\leq n-k
. But this follows from the strong property in
A
. Indeed, looking at the above bases for
B
,
C
and
B\cap C
, it follows that

\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}\}\cup\{v_{m+1},\ldots,v_{k}\}\cup\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{h}\}=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}\cup\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{h}\}

is a basis of
B\vee C
. In particular, this set is independent, and hence
k+h\leq n
, i.e.
h\leq n-k
, as required.
∎

The inequality just established leads to a rigid twisting of
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
. Although this need not be tight in general, it does satisfy one half of axiom
(T2)
.

Theorem 4.8
.

Suppose
A
is a strong independence algebra of finite dimension
n
.

(i)

The partial endomorphism monoid
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
has a twisting given by

\Phi(a,b)=n-\operatorname{rank}(a)-\operatorname{rank}(b)+\operatorname{rank}(ab).

(ii)

This twisting satisfies
(T2)
(T2)(b)
. That is, for any
a,b\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
we have
ab=ab^{\prime}
for some
b^{\prime}\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
with
\Phi(a,b^{\prime})=0
.

(iii)

In the previous part, if
b
belongs to
\operatorname{End}(A)
or to
\operatorname{PAut}(A)
, then such an element
b^{\prime}
exists in
\operatorname{End}(A)
or
\operatorname{PAut}(A)
, respectively.

Proof.

(i)
. This follows from Lemma
4.4
and Proposition
4.6
.

(ii)
. Fix
a,b\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
, and let
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
and
B,C\leq A
be as in the proof of Proposition
4.6
. Also fix the bases

\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{l}\}
of
\operatorname{im}(ab)
,
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}\}
of
B\cap C
and
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}
of
B
,

as in the same proof, where
l\leq m\leq k
, and where
v_{i}b=w_{i}
for each
1\leq i\leq l
. Now extend
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}
to a basis
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\}
of
A
, and extend
\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{l}\}
to an independent set
\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{l}\}\cup\{z_{k+1},\ldots,z_{n}\}
. (The latter is possible because
l\leq k
.) Let

D=\langle v_{1},\ldots,v_{m},v_{k+1},\ldots,v_{n}\rangle\and E=\langle w_{1},\ldots,w_{l},z_{k+1},\ldots,z_{n}\rangle,

and define
b^{\prime}:D\to E
in
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
by

v_{i}b^{\prime}=v_{i}b\text{ for $1\leq i\leq m$}\and v_{j}b^{\prime}=z_{j}\text{ for $k+1\leq j\leq n$.}

Note that
b^{\prime}
agrees with
b
on the basis
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}\}\cup\{v_{m+1},\ldots,v_{k}\}
of
B=\operatorname{im}(a)
, in the sense that
v_{i}b
and
v_{i}b^{\prime}
are equal for
1\leq i\leq m
, but are both undefined for
m+1\leq i\leq k
. It follows from this that
ab=ab^{\prime}
. By construction, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{rank}(b^{\prime})=\dim(\operatorname{im}(b^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\dim\langle v_{1}b,\ldots,v_{m}b,z_{k+1},\ldots,z_{n}\rangle

\displaystyle=\dim\langle w_{1},\ldots,w_{l},z_{k+1},\ldots,z_{n}\rangle=l+n-k,

where we used the fact that

\langle w_{1},\ldots,w_{l}\rangle=\operatorname{im}(ab)=(B\cap C)b=\langle v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}\rangle b=\langle v_{1}b,\ldots,v_{m}b\rangle.

It follows that indeed

\Phi(a,b^{\prime})=n-k-(l+n-k)+l=0.

(iii)
. Suppose first that
b\in\operatorname{PAut}(A)
. We must then have
l=m
, as
b
maps
B\cap C=\langle v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}\rangle
bijectively onto
\langle w_{1},\ldots,w_{l}\rangle
. It follows from this that

\dim(D)=m+n-k=l+n-k=\dim(E),

so that
b^{\prime}:D\to E
is indeed an isomorphism, and hence belongs to
\operatorname{PAut}(A)
.

On the other hand, if
b\in\operatorname{End}(A)
, then
C=\operatorname{dom}(b)=A
, and so
B\cap C=B
, which forces
m=k
. We then have

\dim(D)=m+n-k=k+n-k=n,

so that
\operatorname{dom}(b^{\prime})=D=A
, and
b^{\prime}\in\operatorname{End}(A)
.
∎

Remark 4.9
.

The assumption that the independence algebra
A
is
strong
was used in the proof of Proposition
4.6
, which itself feeds into the proof of part
(i)
of Theorem
4.8
. However, this assumption on
A
was not used in the proofs of parts
(ii)
or
(iii)
of the theorem.

Remark 4.10
.

To see that
(T2)
(T2)(a)
does not hold (in general) for the twisting
\Phi
of
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
from Theorem
4.8
, consider the case that
A=\{1,2,3\}
with no operations, so that
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{3}
is the partial transformation semigroup of degree
3
. Then with

a=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2&3\\
1&1&3\end{smallmatrix}\right)\and b=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2&3\\
1&2&2\end{smallmatrix}\right),

both from
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{3}
, we have
ab=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2&3\\
1&1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. The only other element
a^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{3}
satisfying
ab=a^{\prime}b
is
a^{\prime}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2&3\\
1&1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)(=ab)
, and we have
\Phi(a,b)=\Phi(a^{\prime},b)=1
.

The twisting of
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
from Theorem
4.8
restricts to a twisting on any submonoid of
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
. The next result concerns two of the most important such submonoids, namely
\operatorname{End}(A)
and
\operatorname{PAut}(A)
. Since the latter is inverse, the inversion map
a\mapsto a^{-1}
is an involution.

Proposition 4.11
.

If
A
is a strong independence algebra of finite dimension
n
, then
\operatorname{End}(A)
and
\operatorname{PAut}(A)
have twistings given by

\Phi(a,b)=n-\operatorname{rank}(a)-\operatorname{rank}(b)+\operatorname{rank}(ab).

This is tight and
-1
-symmetric for
\operatorname{PAut}(A)
.

Proof.

It remains only to prove the assertion concerning
\operatorname{PAut}(A)
. Symmetry follows from Lemma
4.4
and the identity
\operatorname{rank}(a)=\operatorname{rank}(a^{-1})
. Tightness then follows from Lemma
3.7
and Theorem
4.8
(iii)
.
∎

Remark 4.12
.

For
A=\{1,2,3\}
with no operations, the failure of
(T2)
(T2)(a)
for
\operatorname{End}(A)=\mathcal{T}_{3}
can be deduced from Remark
4.10
, as the elements
a,b\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{3}=\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
used there actually belong to
\mathcal{T}_{3}
.

As special cases, consider again the algebra
A=\{1,\ldots,n\}
with no operations. Here we have

\operatorname{PEnd}(A)=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n},\hskip 20.00003pt\operatorname{End}(A)=\mathcal{T}_{n}\and\operatorname{PAut}(A)=\mathcal{I}_{n}.

It follows from Proposition
4.11
that the twisting on
\mathcal{I}_{n}
is tight, but we observed in Remarks
4.10
and
4.12
that those on
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}
and
\mathcal{T}_{n}
are loose. The next result gives an important special case in which the twisting on
\operatorname{End}(A)

is
tight.
It is well known that the linear monoid
M_{n}(F)
, consisting of all
n\times n
matrices over an arbitrary field
F
, under multiplication, is isomorphic to
\operatorname{End}(V)
for any
n
-dimensional vector space
V
over
F
, and that
V
is a strong independence algebra (of dimension
n
). It is also well known that the transpose map
a\mapsto a^{T}
is an involution on
M_{n}(F)
.

Proposition 4.13
.

For any
n\geq 1
and any field
F
, the linear monoid
M_{n}(F)
has a tight,
T
-symmetric twisting, given by

\Phi(a,b)=n-\operatorname{rank}(a)-\operatorname{rank}(b)+\operatorname{rank}(ab).

Proof.

By Proposition
4.11
, and the above-mentioned isomorphism
M_{n}(F)\cong\operatorname{End}(V)
, this
\Phi
is a twisting.
Since
\operatorname{rank}(a^{T})=\operatorname{rank}(a)
for all
a\in M_{n}(F)
, we again obtain
T
-symmetry from Lemma
4.4
. Thus, another appeal to Lemma
3.7
and Theorem
4.8
shows that
\Phi
is tight.
∎

4.4
New twistings for diagram monoids

We now prove a Sylvester-style rank inequality for partition monoids, which will allow us to introduce a new family of (rigid) twistings for diagram monoids; see Theorem
4.17
. Curiously, these are tight for the Brauer monoids
\mathcal{B}_{n}
, but not for any of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
,
\mathcal{M}_{n}
or
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
(apart from trivially small
n
), as we show in Proposition
4.18
. Before we begin, we need a technical result concerning joins of equivalence relations.

Let
\mathfrak{Eq}(X)
be the
\vee
-semilattice of equivalence relations on a finite set
X
.
For
\varepsilon\in\mathfrak{Eq}(X)
we write
\|\varepsilon\|=|X/\varepsilon|
for the number of
\varepsilon
-classes.
For distinct
x,y\in X
, let
\varepsilon_{xy}
be the equivalence whose only non-trivial class is
\{x,y\}
. Note then that for any
\varepsilon\in\mathfrak{Eq}(X)
, the join
\varepsilon\vee\varepsilon_{xy}
is either equal to
\varepsilon
or else is obtained by merging precisely two
\varepsilon
-classes. Thus, we have

\|\varepsilon\vee\varepsilon_{xy}\|\geq\|\varepsilon\|-1.

(4.14)

Lemma 4.15
.

If
\varepsilon,\eta\in\mathfrak{Eq}(X)
for a finite set
X
, then
\|\varepsilon\|+\|\eta\|\leq\|\varepsilon\vee\eta\|+|X|
.

Proof.

Write
k=\|\varepsilon\|
,
l=\|\eta\|
and
n=|X|
. We show by descending induction on
l
that
\|\varepsilon\vee\eta\|\geq k+l-n
. If
l=n
, then
\eta=\Delta_{X}
, and the claim is obvious since
\varepsilon\vee\eta=\varepsilon
. So now suppose
l<n
. We can then write
\eta=\eta^{\prime}\vee\varepsilon_{xy}
for some
\eta^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{Eq}(X)
with
\|\eta^{\prime}\|=l+1
, and for distinct
x,y\in X
. Using (
4.14
) and induction, we then have

\|\varepsilon\vee\eta\|=\|\varepsilon\vee\eta^{\prime}\vee\varepsilon_{xy}\|\geq\|\varepsilon\vee\eta^{\prime}\|-1\geq(k+(l+1)-n)-1=k+l-n.\qed

Remark 4.16
.

Lemma
4.15
says that when
|X|=n
is finite, the semilattice
\mathfrak{Eq}(X)
satisfies the Sylvester-type inequality (
4.5
) with
m=n
and
r(\varepsilon)=\|\varepsilon\|
. It then follows from Lemma
4.4
that
\mathfrak{Eq}(X)
has a (rigid) twisting given by

\Phi(\varepsilon,\eta)=n-\|\varepsilon\|-\|\eta\|+\|\varepsilon\vee\eta\|.

This is easily seen to be tight. Indeed, let
\varepsilon,\eta\in\mathfrak{Eq}(X)
, and let
k=\|\varepsilon\|
and
l=\|\varepsilon\vee\eta\|
. Also let
{\bf n}/(\varepsilon\vee\eta)=\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{l}\}
. Now, each
A_{i}
is a union of
\varepsilon
-classes, say
A_{i}=A_{i1}\cup\cdots\cup A_{ik_{i}}
.
For each
1\leq i\leq l
and
1\leq j\leq k_{i}
, choose some
a_{ij}\in A_{ij}
, and let
\eta^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{Eq}(X)
be such that
\{a_{ij}:1\leq j\leq k_{i}\}
is an
\eta^{\prime}
-class for each
1\leq i\leq l
, with every other
\eta^{\prime}
-class a singleton. Then

\varepsilon\vee\eta^{\prime}=\varepsilon\vee\eta\and\|\eta^{\prime}\|=l+n-(k_{1}+\cdots+k_{l})=l+n-k,

so that
\Phi(\varepsilon,\eta^{\prime})=n-k-(l+n-k)+l=0
.

We now return to the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
:

Theorem 4.17
.

For any
a,b\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
we have

\operatorname{rank}(a)+\operatorname{rank}(b)\leq\operatorname{rank}(ab)+n.

Consequently,
\mathcal{P}_{n}
has a
*
-symmetric twisting given by

\Phi(a,b)=n-\operatorname{rank}(a)-\operatorname{rank}(b)+\operatorname{rank}(ab).

Proof.

Because of Lemma
4.4
and the identity
\operatorname{rank}(a^{*})=\operatorname{rank}(a)
, it suffices to prove the claimed inequality.
So fix
a,b\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, and write
k=\operatorname{rank}(a)
,
l=\operatorname{rank}(b)
and
m=\operatorname{rank}(ab)
; we must show that
m\geq k+l-n
.

During the proof we will be interested in three kinds of connected components of the product graph
\Pi(a,b)
. Specifically, we call a component
C
:

•

a
transversal
if
C\cap{\bf n}\not=\varnothing
and
C\cap{\bf n}^{\prime}\not=\varnothing
(in which case necessarily also
C\cap{\bf n}^{\prime\prime}\not=\varnothing
),

•

an
upper semi-transversal
if
C\cap{\bf n}\not=\varnothing
,
C\cap{\bf n}^{\prime\prime}\not=\varnothing
and
C\cap{\bf n}^{\prime}=\varnothing
,

•

a
lower semi-transversal
if
C\cap{\bf n}=\varnothing
,
C\cap{\bf n}^{\prime\prime}\not=\varnothing
and
C\cap{\bf n}^{\prime}\not=\varnothing
.

Note that for any such
C
, the intersection
C\cap{\bf n}^{\prime\prime}
has the form
A^{\prime\prime}
for some
\operatorname{coker}(a)\vee\ker(b)
-class
A
. Note also that
{m=\operatorname{rank}(ab)}
is equal to the number of transversals of
\Pi(a,b)
. Denote the unions of all transversals, all upper semi-transversals and all lower semi-transversals of
\Pi(a,b)
, respectively, by:

X\cup Y^{\prime\prime}\cup Z^{\prime},\hskip 20.00003ptT\cup U^{\prime\prime}\and V^{\prime\prime}\cup W^{\prime},\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptT,U,V,W,X,Y,Z\subseteq{\bf n}.

By construction, the sets
Y
,
U
and
V
are pairwise disjoint, so it follows that

|U|+|V|+|Y|\leq n.

Now consider the restrictions
\varepsilon=\operatorname{coker}(a){\upharpoonright}_{Y}
and
\eta=\ker(b){\upharpoonright}_{Y}
. For any transversal
C
of
\Pi(a,b)
, we have
C\cap{\bf n}^{\prime\prime}=A^{\prime\prime}
for some
\varepsilon\vee\eta
-class
A
. It follows that

m=\operatorname{rank}(ab)=\|\varepsilon\vee\eta\|.

Also let:

•

k_{1}
(resp.
l_{1}
) be the number of transversals of
a
(resp.
b
) contained in a transversal of
\Pi(a,b)
,

•

k_{2}
(resp.
l_{2}
) be the number of transversals of
a
(resp.
b
) contained in a semi-transversal.

We then have

k=k_{1}+k_{2},\hskip 20.00003ptl=l_{1}+l_{2},\hskip 20.00003ptk_{1}\leq\|\varepsilon\|,\hskip 20.00003ptl_{1}\leq\|\eta\|,\hskip 20.00003ptk_{2}\leq|U|\and l_{2}\leq|V|.

Combining the above information with Lemma
4.15
, it follows that indeed

\displaystyle m=\|\varepsilon\vee\eta\|\geq\|\varepsilon\|+\|\eta\|-|Y|\geq k_{1}+l_{1}-|Y|

\displaystyle=(k-k_{2})+(l-l_{2})-|Y|

\displaystyle\geq k+l-|U|-|V|-|Y|\geq k+l-n.\qed

We call
\Phi
from Theorem
4.17
the
rank-based twisting
of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
. As ever, this restricts to a twisting on any submonoid of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
.

Proposition 4.18
.

The rank-based twisting is tight for
\mathcal{B}_{n}
, but loose for
\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
and
\mathcal{M}_{n}
for
n\geq 2
, and for
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
for
n\geq 3
.

Proof.

For the failure of
(T2)
in
\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
and
\mathcal{M}_{n}
, consider
a,b\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
as in (
4.3
), and note that
ab=b
. For any
b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
with
ab^{\prime}=ab
, we have

\Phi(a,b^{\prime})=n-(n-2)-\operatorname{rank}(b^{\prime})+(n-2)=n-\operatorname{rank}(b^{\prime}).

For this to equal
0
we would need
\operatorname{rank}(b^{\prime})=n
, meaning that
b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
is a permutation. But for any permutation
b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
we have
\operatorname{coker}(ab^{\prime})=\Delta_{\bf n}\not=\operatorname{coker}(b)
, so that
ab^{\prime}\not=b=ab
.

For
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
, consider

a={\lower 6.02773pt\hbox{

}}.

As in the previous paragraph, any
b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
satisfying
ab^{\prime}=ab(=b)
and
\Phi(a,b^{\prime})=0
must have
\operatorname{rank}(b^{\prime})=n
. But there is a unique Temperley–Lieb diagram of rank
n
, namely the identity
1
, and we do not have
a\cdot 1=b
.

By Lemma
3.7
, it remains to verify
(T2)
(T2)(b)
in
\mathcal{B}_{n}
. To do so, fix
{a,b\in\mathcal{B}_{n}}
, let
k=\operatorname{rank}(a)
and
l=\operatorname{rank}(ab)
, and write

a=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil x_{1}&\cdots&x_{l}&x_{l+1}&\cdots&x_{k}&x_{k+1},x_{k+2}&\cdots&x_{n-1},x_{n}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil y_{1}&\cdots&y_{l}&y_{l+1}&\cdots&y_{k}&y_{k+1},y_{k+2}&\cdots&y_{n-1},y_{n}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

Re-ordering if necessary, we can assume that
ab
has the form

ab=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil x_{1}&\cdots&x_{l}&x_{l+1},x_{l+2}&\cdots&x_{k-1},x_{k}&x_{k+1},x_{k+2}&\cdots&x_{n-1},x_{n}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil z_{1}&\cdots&z_{l}&z_{l+1},z_{l+2}&\cdots&z_{k-1},z_{k}&z_{k+1},z_{k+2}&\cdots&z_{n-1},z_{n}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

We then have
ab=ab^{\prime}
for

b^{\prime}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil y_{1}&\cdots&y_{l}&y_{k+1}&\cdots&y_{n}&y_{l+1},y_{l+2}&\cdots&y_{k-1},y_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil z_{1}&\cdots&z_{l}&z_{k+1}&\cdots&z_{n}&z_{l+1},z_{l+2}&\cdots&z_{k-1},z_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr),

and
\Phi(a,b^{\prime})=n-k-(l+n-k)+l=0
.
∎

Remark 4.19
.

The rank-based twisting coincides with the canonical (float-counting) twisting on the submonoid
\mathcal{I}_{n}
of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
. Indeed, consider
a,b\in\mathcal{I}_{n}
. A floating component in the product graph
\Pi(a,b)
is simply a point
x^{\prime\prime}
, and there is one for each
x\in{\bf n}\setminus(\operatorname{codom}(a)\cup\operatorname{dom}(b))
. Thus, writing
\Phi
for the canonical twisting, we have

\displaystyle\Phi(a,b)

\displaystyle=n-|\operatorname{codom}(a)\cup\operatorname{dom}(b)|

\displaystyle=n-|\operatorname{codom}(a)|-|\operatorname{dom}(b)|+|\operatorname{codom}(a)\cap\operatorname{dom}(b)|

\displaystyle=n-\operatorname{rank}(a)-\operatorname{rank}(b)+\operatorname{rank}(ab).

The rank-based and canonical twistings are distinct for the monoids
\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
,
\mathcal{B}_{n}
,
\mathcal{M}_{n}
and
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
, however. In fact, we claim that for any of these monoids, the canonical twisting
\Phi
is not rigid. That is, it does not have the form
\Phi_{r,m}
(cf. Lemma
4.4
) for
any
function
r
and integer
m
(apart from trivially small
n
). Indeed, consider any submonoid
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}\leq S\leq\mathcal{P}_{n}
for
n\geq 3
, and suppose to the contrary that
\Phi=\Phi_{r,m}
for some
r:S\to\mathbb{Z}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. Note then that for any idempotent
e\in E(S)
we have

\Phi(e,e)=\Phi_{r,m}(e,e)=m-r(e)-r(e)+r(ee)=m-r(e),\hskip 20.00003pt\text{so that}\hskip 20.00003ptr(e)=m-\Phi(e,e).

Now consider the idempotents

}},

all from
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}(\subseteq S)
. Since
\Phi(e,e)=\Phi(f,f)=1
and
\Phi(ef,ef)=0
, we then have

0=\Phi(e,f)=\Phi_{r,m}(e,f)=m-r(e)-r(f)+r(ef)=m-(m-1)-(m-1)+m=2,

a contradiction.

Remark 4.20
.

Some products arising from the rank-based twistings are shown in the right-hand columns of Figures
3
–
5
for
\mathcal{P}_{2}
,
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{2}
,
\mathcal{B}_{3}
and
\mathcal{B}_{4}
. These are loose for
\mathcal{P}_{2}
and
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{2}
, but tight for
\mathcal{B}_{3}
and
\mathcal{B}_{4}
. More information about these diagrams will be presented in Remark
5.3
.

5
Green’s relations

The rest of the paper is devoted to describing the structure and properties of a tight twisted product
{M\times_{\Phi}^{q}S}
. We begin here with a description of Green’s relations.
Throughout this section, we fix a monoid
S
, a tight twisting
\Phi:S\times S\to\mathbb{N}
, and an (additive) commutative monoid
M
with a fixed element
q\in M
. To simplify notation, we will denote the arising twisted product
M\times_{\Phi}^{q}S
by
T
.

The next result shows how Green’s relations on
T=M\times_{\Phi}^{q}S
are built from the corresponding relations on
M
and
S
. To avoid confusion, we write
\xi^{M}
,
\xi^{S}
and
\xi^{T}
for the
\xi
-relation on
M
,
S
and
T
, respectively, where here
\xi
is any of Green’s relations (pre-orders or equivalences).
Note that
{\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}^{M}}={\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}^{M}}={\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}^{M}}={\operatorname{\mathscr{D}}^{M}}={\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}^{M}}
and
{\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}}^{M}}={\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}}^{M}}={\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}^{M}}={\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}}^{M}}
, because of commutativity.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
T=M\times_{\Phi}^{q}S
be a tight twisted product.
If
\xi
is any of Green’s relations (pre-orders or equivalences), then

(i,a)\mathrel{\xi}^{T}(j,b)\ \ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ \ i\mathrel{\xi}^{M}j\ \ \text{and}\ \ a\mathrel{\xi}^{S}b\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $i,j\in M$ and $a,b\in S$.}

Proof.

We just prove the result when
\xi
is
\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}
. The proofs for
\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}}
and
\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}}
are analogous, and then the remaining ones follow by combining these.

For the forward implication, suppose
(i,a)\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}^{T}(j,b)
, so that

(i,a)=(k,s)(j,b)(l,t)=(j+k+l+\Phi(s,b,t)q,sbt)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $k,l\in M$ and $s,t\in S$.}

Then
i=j+k+l+\Phi(s,b,t)q\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}^{M}j
and
a=sbt\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}^{S}b
.

Conversely, suppose
i\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}^{M}j
and
a\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}^{S}b
, so that
i=j+l
for some
l\in M
(recall that
M
is commutative) and
a=sbt
for some
s,t\in S
. By
(T6)
we can assume that
\Phi(s,b,t)=0
. We then have

(0,s)(j,b)(l,t)=(j+l+\Phi(s,b,t)q,sbt)=(i+0q,a)=(i,a),

and so
{(i,a)\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}^{T}(j,b)}
.
∎

We state some immediate consequences:

Corollary 5.2
.

Let
T=M\times_{\Phi}^{q}S
be a tight twisted product, and
\operatorname{\mathscr{K}}
any of Green’s equivalences.

(i)

For any
i\in M
and
a\in S
we have
K_{(i,a)}^{T}=K_{i}^{M}\times K_{a}^{S}=H_{i}^{M}\times K_{a}^{S}
.

(ii)

For
{\operatorname{\mathscr{K}}}\neq{\operatorname{\mathscr{D}}}
we have the poset isomorphism

(T/{\operatorname{\mathscr{K}}^{T}},\leq)\cong(M/{\operatorname{\mathscr{K}}^{M}},\leq)\times(S/{\operatorname{\mathscr{K}}^{S}},\leq)=(M/{\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}^{M}},\leq)\times(S/{\operatorname{\mathscr{K}}^{S}},\leq).

In particular, if
M
is a group, then
K_{(i,a)}^{T}=M\times K_{a}^{S}
, and
(T/{\operatorname{\mathscr{K}}^{T}},\leq)\cong(S/{\operatorname{\mathscr{K}}^{S}},\leq)
for
{\operatorname{\mathscr{K}}}\not={\operatorname{\mathscr{D}}}
. ∎

Remark 5.3
.

We now discuss some examples of tight twisted products, to illustrate Theorem
5.1
and Corollary
5.2
. For comparison we also consider some loose products, where these results no longer apply. All examples will involve the two-element additive monoid
M=\{0,\infty\}
, and we will take
q=\infty
. In each case
S
is a diagram monoid of small degree. The tightness or looseness of the considered twistings always follows from Proposition
4.2
or
4.18
.
The Green structure for the loose examples was computed using the Semigroups package for GAP
[
30
,
45
]
.

We start with
S=\mathcal{P}_{2}
, the partition monoid of degree
2
, whose egg-box diagram is shown in Figure
3
(left). The tight twisted product
T=M\times_{\Phi}^{\infty}S
, arising from the canonical twisting
\Phi
, is shown in Figure
3
(middle). Here blue and red diagrams of
a\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
represent
(0,a)
and
(\infty,a)
, respectively, and group
\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}
-classes are shaded grey; the same colouring conventions apply to the other diagrams discussed below.
The posets
(S/{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}^{S}},\leq)\cong{\bf 3}
and
{(M/{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}^{M}},\leq)\cong{\bf 2}}
are chains of sizes
3
and
2
, respectively, while
(T/{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}^{T}},\leq)\cong{\bf 3}\times{\bf 2}
is the direct product, as can be readily seen in the diagram.

By contrast, Figure
3
(right) pictures the egg-box diagram of the twisted product
M\times_{\Phi}^{\infty}\mathcal{P}_{2}
, where this time
\Phi
is the rank-based twisting. Since this twisting is loose, Theorem
5.1
does not apply here, and one can immediately see from the diagram that the
\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}
-class poset is not isomorphic to the direct product
{\bf 3}\times{\bf 2}
. Indeed, while the sets
\{0\}\times D_{1}
and
\{0\}\times D_{0}
are whole
\operatorname{\mathscr{D}}
-classes in the product arising from the canonical twisting, they ‘fall apart’ into multiple
\operatorname{\mathscr{D}}
-classes for the rank-based twisting.

Figure
4
follows the same pattern for the partial Brauer monoid
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{2}
, showing egg-box diagrams for
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{2}
itself (left), and the products
M\times_{\Phi}^{\infty}\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{2}
arising from the canonical twisting (middle) and the rank-based twisting (right). Both of these products are loose. Although they have the same
{\operatorname{\mathscr{D}}}={\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}
-classes as each other, the orderings on these are different.

Figure
5
treats the Brauer monoids
\mathcal{B}_{3}
(top row) and
\mathcal{B}_{4}
(bottom row). For reasons of space, we have not pictured individual elements here;
\mathcal{B}_{4}
has size
105
. The canonical and rank-based twistings are both tight, and in each case the poset of
\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}
-classes decomposes as a direct product.

Figure 3:
Egg-box diagrams of the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{2}
, and two of its twisted products. See Remark
5.3
for more details.

Figure 4:
Egg-box diagrams of the partial Brauer monoid
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{2}
, and two of its twisted products. See Remark
5.3
for more details.

Figure 5:
Egg-box diagrams of the Brauer monoids
\mathcal{B}_{3}
(top row) and
\mathcal{B}_{4}
(bottom row), and two each of their twisted products. See Remark
5.3
for more details.

As an application of the above results, we can also deduce a useful result concerning the property of stability.
A semigroup
S
is said to be
stable
if

a\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}ab\ \Leftrightarrow\ a\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}ab\and a\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}ba\ \Leftrightarrow\ a\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}ba\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $a,b\in S$.}

(5.4)

Stable semigroups have many important and useful properties, as explained in
[
23
]
. It is known that all finite semigroups are stable, and that
{\operatorname{\mathscr{D}}}={\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}
in any stable semigroup; see for example
[
50
, Section A.2]
. Any commutative semigroup is stable because for such a semigroup we have
{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}={\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}}={\operatorname{\mathscr{L}}}
.

Proposition 5.5
.

A tight twisted product
T=M\times_{\Phi}^{q}S
is stable if and only if
S
is stable.

Proof.

Throughout the proof we use Theorem
5.1
extensively, without explicit mention.
Suppose first that
S
is stable. To demonstrate stability of
T
, it suffices by symmetry to establish the first equivalence in (
5.4
), and since
{\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}}\subseteq{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}
, only the forward implication is required. So suppose

(i,a)\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}^{T}(i,a)(j,b)=(i+j+\Phi(a,b)q,ab)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $i,j\in M$ and $a,b\in S$.}

We then have
i\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}^{M}i+j+\Phi(a,b)q
and
a\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}^{S}ab
. But
M
and
S
are both stable (by assumption for
S
, and by commutativity for
M
), so it follows that
i\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}^{M}i+j+\Phi(a,b)q
and
a\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}^{S}ab
. But this then gives

(i,a)\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}^{T}(i+j+\Phi(a,b)q,ab)=(i,a)(j,b).

Conversely, suppose
T
is stable. Again aiming to prove the forward implication in the first equivalence in (
5.4
), suppose
a\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}^{S}ab
for some
a,b\in S
. By
(T2)
(T2)(b)
, we can assume that
{\Phi(a,b)=0}
. We then have

(0,a)\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}^{T}(0,ab)=(0,a)(0,b),

so it follows from stability of
T
that
(0,a)\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}^{T}(0,a)(0,b)=(0,ab)
, and hence that
a\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}^{S}ab
.
∎

In
[
40
, Corollary 3]
it is proved that the twisted Brauer monoid
\mathbb{Z}\times_{\Phi}^{1}\mathcal{B}_{n}
is stable when
\Phi
is the canonical twisting. This also follows from Proposition
5.5
because
\mathcal{B}_{n}
is finite and hence stable.

6
Idempotents, Schützenberger groups and biordered sets

We continue to fix a tight twisted product
T=M\times_{\Phi}^{q}S
, where
M
,
S
,
\Phi
and
q
have their usual meanings.
In this section we describe the set
E(T)
of idempotents of
T
(Section
6.1
), and its structure as a biordered set (Section
6.3
).
In between, we show how the Schützenberger groups of
T
are related to those of
M
and
S
(Section
6.2
).

6.1
The idempotents

Before we look at the idempotents of the tight twisted product
T=M\times_{\Phi}^{q}S
directly, it is convenient to describe the group
\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}^{T}
-classes. We begin with a technical lemma concerning the additive monoid
(M,+)
, whose simple proof is omitted; for similar results see
[
32
, Section I.4]
.

Lemma 6.1
.

If
H
is a group
\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}^{M}
-class of
M
, and if
k\in M
, then the following are equivalent:

(i)

H=H+k
,

(ii)

i\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}^{M}k
for some
i\in H
,

(iii)

i\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}^{M}k
for all
i\in H
. ∎

If
H
is an
\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}^{S}
-class (of
S
), then it quickly follows from
(T3)
and
(T4)
that
\Phi(a,b)=\Phi(c,d)
for all
a,b,c,d\in H
. We write
\Phi(H)
for this common value (so
\Phi(H)=\Phi(a,b)
for any
a,b\in H
).

In the following proof, we will make use of the fact that an
\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}
-class
H
of a semigroup is a group if and only if
xy\in H
for some
x,y\in H
; see for example
[
36
, Theorem 2.2.5]
.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
T=M\times_{\Phi}^{q}S
be a tight twisted product. If
H
is an
\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}^{M}
-class, and
H^{\prime}
an
\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}^{S}
-class, then the following are equivalent:

(i)

the
\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}^{T}
-class
H\times H^{\prime}
is a group,

(ii)

H
and
H^{\prime}
are groups, and
H=H+\Phi(H^{\prime})q
,

(iii)

H
and
H^{\prime}
are groups, and
i\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}^{M}\Phi(H^{\prime})q
for some (equivalently all)
i\in H
,

(iv)

H
and
H^{\prime}
are groups, and either
\Phi(H^{\prime})=0
or else
i\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}^{M}q
for some (equivalently all)
{i\in H}
.

When the above conditions hold, the group
\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}^{T}
-class
H\times H^{\prime}
is isomorphic to the direct product
(H,+)\times(H^{\prime},\cdot)
.

Proof.

(i)
\ \Rightarrow\
(iii)
. Suppose
H\times H^{\prime}
is a group, say with identity
(i,a)
. Since

(i,a)=(i,a)^{2}=(2i+\Phi(a,a)q,a^{2}),

it follows that
a=a^{2}
and
i=2i+\Phi(a,a)q=2i+\Phi(H^{\prime})q
. The former tells us that
H_{a}^{S}=H^{\prime}
is a group. The latter tells us that
i\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}^{M}\Phi(H^{\prime})q
, and also that
i\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}^{M}2i
. Since
{\operatorname{\mathscr{R}}^{M}}={\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}^{M}}
, this gives
i\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}^{M}2i
, which implies that
H_{i}^{M}=H
is a group (as mentioned before the proof).

(iii)
\ \Rightarrow\
(ii)
. This follows from Lemma
6.1
.

(ii)
\ \Rightarrow\
(i)
.
Suppose
H
and
H^{\prime}
are groups, with
H=H+\Phi(H^{\prime})q
. Let the identities of these groups be
i\in H
and
e\in H^{\prime}
, and let
p\in H
be such that
i=p+\Phi(H^{\prime})q=p+\Phi(e,e)q
. Since
i
is the identity of
H
, we have

(p,e)(p,e)=(2p+\Phi(e,e)q,e^{2})=(p+i,e)=(p,e),

and hence
(p,e)\in H\times H^{\prime}
is an idempotent.

(iii)
\ \Rightarrow\
(iv)
. This is clear.

(iv)
\ \Rightarrow\
(iii)
.
We only need to check that
i\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}^{M}\Phi(H^{\prime})q
. If
\Phi(H^{\prime})=0
this is obvious.
When
\Phi(H^{\prime})\neq 0
we have
\Phi(H^{\prime})i\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}^{M}i
because
H
is a group, and hence

i\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}^{M}\Phi(H^{\prime})i\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}^{M}\Phi(H^{\prime})q
, as requied.

For the final assertion, suppose
H\times H^{\prime}
is a group. Let
i
and
e
be the identities of the groups
H
and
H^{\prime}
, respectively, and write
m=\Phi(H^{\prime})
, so that

i\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}^{M}mq,\hskip 20.00003ptH=H+mq\and\Phi(a,b)=m\text{ for all $a,b\in H^{\prime}$.}

Also let
t=i+mq
; since
H=H+mq
we have
t\in H
. For any
j,k\in H
and
a,b\in H^{\prime}
we have

(j,a)(k,b)=(j+k+\Phi(a,b)q,ab)=(j+k+i+mq,ab)=(j+t+k,ab).

It follows that the
\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}^{T}
-class
H\times H^{\prime}
is isomorphic to the direct product
(H,\oplus)\times(H^{\prime},\cdot)
, where
\oplus
is the
variant
operation
[
34
]
given by
j\oplus k=j+t+k
. Since the map
j\mapsto j+t
is easily seen to be an isomorphism
(H,\oplus)\to(H,+)
, the proof is complete.
∎

We now define the set

\begin{split}\Omega&=\big\{(i,e)\in E(M)\times E(S):H_{i}^{M}\times H_{e}^{S}\text{ is a group}\big\}\\
&=\big\{(i,e)\in E(M)\times E(S):i\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}^{M}\Phi(e,e)q\big\},\end{split}

(6.3)

where the second equality follows from Theorem
6.2
. For
(i,e)\in\Omega
, we write
\varepsilon(i,e)
for the unique idempotent in
H_{i}^{M}\times H_{e}^{S}
, which is of course the identity element of this group
\operatorname{\mathscr{H}}^{T}
-class. The next result follows from Theorem
6.2
and its proof.

Proposition 6.4
.

We have
E(T)=E(M\times_{\Phi}^{q}S)=\{\varepsilon(i,e):(i,e)\in\Omega\}
, where
\Omega
is as in (
6.3
). For any
(i,e)\in\Omega
, we have

\varepsilon(i,e)=(p,e),\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptp=p(i,e)\in H_{i}^{M}\text{ is such that $i=p+\Phi(e,e)q$.}

Remark 6.5
.

If
q
is a unit, then so too is
\Phi(e,e)q
for any
e\in E(S)
. In this case it follows that
\Omega=E(M)\times E(S)
, and that

\varepsilon(i,e)=(i-\Phi(e,e)q,e)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $i\in E(M)$ and $e\in E(S)$.}

In particular, if
M
is a group, so that
E(M)=\{0\}
, the idempotents of
T
all have the form
\varepsilon(0,e)=(-\Phi(e,e)q,e)
for
e\in E(S)
.

On the other hand, if
e\in E(S)
is such that
\Phi(e,e)=0
, then
(i,e)\in\Omega
for all
i\in E(M)
. In this case we have
p(i,e)=i
, and so
\varepsilon(i,e)=(i,e)
.

If
M
is unipotent, i.e.
E(M)=\{0\}
, but
q
is not a unit, then
0\not\leq_{\operatorname{\mathscr{J}}}^{M}q
, and so

\Omega=\{(0,e):e\in E(S),\ \Phi(e,e)=0\}=E(T).

Remark 6.6
.

Figure
3
pictures the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{2}
(left) as well as the tight twisted product
T=M\times_{\Phi}^{\infty}\mathcal{P}_{2}
(middle), where
M=\{0,\infty\}
, and where
\Phi
is the canonical twisting. Since
\infty
is an absorbing element of
M
, we see that
(\infty,e)
is an idempotent of
T
for every idempotent
e
of
\mathcal{P}_{2}
. On the other hand,
(0,e)
is an idempotent of
T
for

but not for

from

, as
\Phi(e,e)=0
and
\Phi(f,f)=1
. On the other hand, for the rank-based twisting we have
\Phi(e,e)=4-\operatorname{rank}(e)>0
for any idempotent
e\in E(\mathcal{B}_{4})
of rank less than
